For item 3 (GPU evaluation):
- R26 gave Overall 3.0. The issues were:


- SF1 through SF6 are all fixed now, along with the weaknesses around F1 gaps and PL theory novelty, plus the major findings about total violation numbers.
- M2 and M4 were just informational issues I could skip.
- The experimental design is actually quite comprehensive—I've got three model families tested across two RE datasets with zero-shot and fine-tuning variants, constraint ablations, beam search sweeps, quantization robustness checks, prompt sensitivity analysis, 70B scaling studies, and detailed error breakdowns across relations and document lengths.
- Now I'm thinking about what additional experiments could strengthen this further, starting with cross-family comparisons.

For the remaining time, I'm weighing the tradeoffs: Qwen-72B would solidify the 70B narrative but requires substantial GPU resources that might not be worth the risk. ACE05 would need too much pipeline setup. Mistral LoRA could help with C1 but Mistral's already the weakest performer. The M2 bootstrap CI is doable quickly since it's just statistics, not GPU-intensive.

But honestly, the real bottleneck isn't experiments—it's writing quality. The R26 reviewer's feedback was all about clarity and precision, which I've already addressed. Given the time constraints, improving the narrative around what I've already tested is going to have much higher impact than rushing into new experiments.

So I'm going to have the Paper Agent and Reviewer work in parallel to polish the submission while I focus on what's actually going to move the needle.

[Reviewer 回复]:
[severity: info] 提交前全文审查报告 — 三维度评估

## 1. Claim 强度评估

### 当前 claim 强度合理，有 2 个被低估的数据点

**已充分利用的核心 claim：**
- DR≤0.21% + R-F1 ±0.3pp：数据充分，表述准确 ✓
- 非加性组合 3%+24%→94%：论文最独特发现，§7 已正确强调 ✓
- beam k=4 消除所有违规 at 8B：准确，有 "at 8B" 限定 ✓
- 70B 84% vs 8B 24%："preliminary single-family" 限定恰当 ✓

**被低估的 2 个数据点（不需要新实验，只需要写作强调）：**

1. **"被约束移除的 triples 中 97.6-100% 是 false positives"**（§5 L88 括号里一笔带过）
   - 这是论文最强的"免费午餐"论据：约束几乎不损失任何正确预测
   - 当前位置：§5 composability 段括号内 "removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives"
   - 建议：提升到 §1 intro 中，与 "R-F1 ±0.3pp" 并列。"Constraints are effectively free: 97.6-100% of removed triples are false positives, with only 1/514 (0.2%) false negatives blocked"
   - 对审稿人"你的约束会不会伤害召回"的预期攻击是最强回应

2. **"514 个 FN 中仅 1 个 (0.2%) 被约束阻止"**（§5 L146）
   - 与上一点互补：从 FP 和 FN 两侧证明约束无害
   - 当前位置：§5 downstream impact 段
   - 可作为 §1 的一句话增强

**不建议修改：** DDL 46h 内改 abstract/intro 引入新的表述风险大于收益。如果 freeze 前有最后一次编辑机会，优先级是这两个点。但如果已经 freeze，不改也可以。

### Claim 过强/过保守检查

- **过保守**：Abstract "reduces DR to ≤0.21%"——实际 Qwen SciERC 达到 DR=0%，但 ≤0.21% 是跨所有 config 的保守上界，保留是正确的
- **恰当**：所有 70B claim 都有 "preliminary/single-family" 限定 ✓
- **恰当**：F1 范围 "40-46%" 与 PL-Marker 41.6% 对比，不声称 SOTA ✓
- **无过强 claim**

## 2. Story 优化空间

### 叙事线评估：连贯，无断裂

Problem（15-18% violations）→ Gap（GCD 无跨字段感知 / post-hoc 丢弃 15-74%）→ Method（decidability → two-layer）→ Key finding（非加性 3+24→94）→ Scale（70B 84%）→ Deployment tiers（greedy/beam-2/beam-4）

每一步都有数据支撑，逻辑链完整。§7 已正确以实证发现（非加性组合）开头，不是 Proposition 1。

### R26 扣分点修复评估

| 扣分点 | 修复前 | 修复后 | 评估 |
|--------|--------|--------|------|
| Claim 精度 | ">90% total reduction" 无支撑 | "26-52% greedy S1; beam k=4 eliminates all" | ✓ 已修 |
| Abstract 过度 | Raw percentage | Severity-framing | ✓ 已修 |
| 70B single-family | 未标注 | "preliminary single-family" + "speculative hypothesis" | ✓ 已修 |
| Position→name gap | Case 1 不适用 name-based | 补充 PDA 无法随机访问论证 | ✓ 已修 |
| GoLLIE/PL-Marker 数字 | 无来源或来源错误 | ACE05 63.6-70.1% + PL-Marker 41.6% 正确引用 | ✓ 已修 |
| Mistral scope | C1 未区分 ZS/FT | "additionally under LoRA fine-tuning" 限定 | ✓ 已修 |

### 残余攻击面（3 个，按严重性排序）

**A. [中] Table 2 无 error bars / std**
Table 2 是 greedy deterministic 解码（T=0），所以 std=0 是合理的。但审稿人可能不理解这一点。当前 caption 说 "No ΔR-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected)"——bootstrap CI 在正文提及但不在表中。
→ 防御：Table 2 caption 可以加一句 "Greedy decoding (T=0) yields deterministic outputs; reported CIs are bootstrap over documents"

**B. [中] 单家族 70B + 无 GoLLIE 组合实验**
声称 "orthogonal and complementary to GoLLIE" 但没有 GoLLIE+OntoCoDec 实验。审稿人可能要求验证。
→ 防御：Appendix GoLLIE comparison 已经论证了方法论差异，且 GoLLIE 在 SciERC 上 R_F1=0，组合实验无意义。

**C. [低] Greedy S1 总违规减少 26-52%**
这是论文中最弱的数字。审稿人可能问 "constraints 只减少了一半违规？"
→ 已防御：severity-framing（DR irrecoverable → CF correctable）比 raw percentage 更有说服力。beam k=4 作为 ultimate solution 也已提及。

## 3. GPU 实验 ROI 评估

### 总体建议：不跑新实验

DDL 46h 内跑新实验的风险显著高于收益。论文已 submission-ready。新实验可能：
- 产生弱结果（如 Mistral FT 因 calibration 差导致 DR 残余高），反而削弱论文
- 引入新数据不一致（未经充分验证的数字进入论文）
- 挤占 final compile / 匿名仓库推送的时间

### 候选逐个评估

| 候选 | 预期评分提升 | 执行风险 | 时间成本 | 推荐 |
|------|-------------|---------|---------|------|
| Qwen-72B 跨家族 | 高（C3→cross-family） | 高（下载35GB+适配+调试） | 28-32h | ❌ 风险太高 |
| Mistral LoRA | 中（Table 2 +1行） | 中（Mistral ZS 56.9% DR，FT 可能不佳） | 6-8h | ❌ 弱结果风险 |
| ACE05 新数据集 | 中（+1 dataset） | 极高（全新 pipeline） | 12-20h | ❌ 不可能 |
| M2 CF bootstrap CI | 低（error bars） | 极低（纯分析） | 2-3h | ⚠️ 可选但收益小 |
| 70B PS 写入 | 已完成 | 无 | 0h | ✅ 已在 appendix |

### 如果一定要做一件事

M2 CF bootstrap CI 是唯一安全的候选：
- 不涉及 GPU，纯 Python 分析
- 为 Table 2 的 CF 列添加 95% CI
- 执行风险接近零
- 但评分提升有限（R26 未明确要求 CF error bars）

**我的建议：不做。将精力集中在确保 final compile 零错误 + 匿名仓库推送。**

## 总结

论文在当前状态下是 solid Findings-level（3.0-3.5），R26 修复有效提升了写作精度。距离 Conference（4.0）的核心差距是：

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017UFSuaLKXzUiHji6a6uCCn`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 74\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\% independently.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% at R-F1 40--46\% ($\pm$0.3\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\sim$31\% (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).
22	
23	OntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
27	    \item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning, demonstrating DR reduction to $\leq$0.21\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.3\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
28	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.
29	    \item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\ 24\% at 8B (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\%, 1.18$\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\times$.
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
```

    "oldString": "LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones",
    "newString": "LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nConstraint enforcement is effectively lossless: 97.6--100\\% of removed triples are false positives, and only 1 of 514 false negatives (0.2\\%) is attributable to constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:downstream-impact}).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 74\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning, demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

Beam search and TCR approximate this, with integrating consensus into decoding remaining future work. The issue is just those final two words spilling over, which is a pagination artifact from my recent edit.

Now I'm looking more carefully at the surrounding intro text to see if there's additional compression possible. The method description spans lines 17-22 and introduces OntoCoDec as a framework with two layers — a grammar layer that compiles constraints into context-free grammars for JSON structure and vocabulary, and a semantic layer that applies dynamic logit masking at runtime. The description notes a scale-dependent interaction where the semantic layer shows different effectiveness at 8B versus 70B model sizes.

The next section details the framework's key properties: it compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies without requiring constraint-specific training, works with any autoregressive LM that exposes logits, and has been validated across three English datasets with multiple model sizes and tokenizer families.

I'm considering whether to merge the new sentence into the existing text rather than adding it as a separate line, or if I should trim something nearby to make room. The core content here is essential to the contribution, so I need to be careful about what gets compressed. I'm trying to find a cleaner way to integrate the false positive statistics into the existing text rather than adding a separate sentence. The key insight is that I can compress the constraint enforcement effectiveness claim by embedding the 97.6--100% false positive rate directly after the latency metric, then move on to the beam search discussion without breaking the flow. enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones, with fine-tuned performance dropping 76--99% on SciERC and 42--90% on DocRED, while total violations decrease around 31% in zero-shot settings. I'm making the edit now to integrate those changes and tighten up the presentation.

    "newString": "LoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); 97.6--100\\% of removed triples are false positives (\\S\\ref{sec:downstream-impact}). Beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 74\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nConstraint enforcement is effectively lossless: 97.6--100\\% of removed triples are false positives, and only 1 of 514 false negatives (0.2\\%) is attributable to constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:downstream-impact}).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning, demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EmqSQE9pVdeR1muViFcRXj`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to $\leq$\,0.21\% at F1 40--46\%, converting irrecoverable type errors to correctable cross-field inconsistencies (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\times$ latency.
10	In zero-shot settings, DR reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; CF increases are concentrated in hallucinated entities (58.1\%) and decrease with scale and fine-tuning.
11	\end{abstract}
12	
```

{
  "text": "**Title:** OntoCoDec: Type-Constrained Information Extraction via Compiled Schema Constraints\n\n**Abstract:** Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples—where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints. These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints. A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.\n\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to ≤0.21% at F1 40–46%, converting irrecoverable type errors to correctable cross-field inconsistencies (CF ≤1.4%); beam search (k=4) eliminates all violation types at 1.39× latency. In zero-shot settings, DR reductions span 76–99% on SciERC and 42–90% on DocRED; CF increases are concentrated in hallucinated entities (58.1%) and decrease with scale and fine-tuning."
}

67	\clearpage
68	\section*{Limitations}
69	
70	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
71	CF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).
72	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.
73	Entity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.
74	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors (28\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
75	Our empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.
76	We do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.
77	Post-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
78	
79	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
80	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
81	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.
82	Evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.
83	WikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).
84	All main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}).
85	
86	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
87	Our approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex}; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
88	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.
89	Evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
90	While composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.
91	The two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:70b-ablation}).
92	
93	
94	% === Ethics Statement ===
95	\section*{Ethics Statement}
96	
97	This work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.
98	No human subjects were involved in the experiments.
99	The constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.
100	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.\nEntity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; ```text
1	\section{Reproducibility Details}
2	\label{app:reproducibility}
3	
4	\paragraph{Fine-tuning configuration.}
5	All four fine-tuned models (Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on SciERC and DocRED) use identical LoRA hyperparameters: rank $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, no bias, targeting all attention and MLP projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{k\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}, \texttt{o\_proj}, \texttt{gate\_proj}, \texttt{up\_proj}, \texttt{down\_proj}).
6	Training uses AdamW with learning rate $2{\times}10^{-4}$, cosine schedule, warmup ratio $0.1$, effective batch size $16$, bf16 precision, gradient checkpointing, and completion-only loss for 3 epochs.
7	SciERC trains on 1,762 examples (260 dev) for 333 steps; DocRED trains on 3,053 examples (998 dev) for 573 steps.
8	Maximum sequence length is 2,048 (SciERC) and 4,096 (DocRED).
9	All runs use seed 42.
10	
11	\paragraph{Hardware and software.}
12	Training runs on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (98\,GB VRAM).
13	SciERC fine-tuning takes approximately 11 minutes per model; DocRED takes approximately 1.5 hours.
14	Software versions: PyTorch 2.10, Transformers 4.57, PEFT 0.19, TRL 1.1, Accelerate 1.13.
15	
16	\paragraph{Inference time.}
17	Inference timing is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:runtime} (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU, same hardware as training).
18	S1 constrained decoding adds $+$13.1\% wall-clock overhead on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot greedy), lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA fine-tuning, overhead is $+$10.0\% on SciERC.
19	
20	\paragraph{Greedy decoding determinism.}
21	\label{app:greedy-determinism}
22	% exp_id: d067_seed_variance
23	All main results use greedy decoding (temperature$\,{=}\,$0, argmax token selection).
24	Argmax is deterministic: the random seed controls \texttt{torch.manual\_seed} for sampling and dropout, neither of which is active during greedy inference.
25	We verify this with three seeds (42, 123, 456) across two LoRA fine-tuned models on all 551 SciERC documents.
26	Seeds 123 and 456, evaluated on the same GPU, produce byte-identical outputs for both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.
27	The residual variance in Table~\ref{tab:seed-variance} stems entirely from cross-hardware floating-point divergence: seed 42 was evaluated on a different server with a different CUDA driver version.
28	Even under this conservative upper bound, $\sigma(\text{E-F1}) \leq 0.16$\,pp, $\sigma(\text{R-F1}) \leq 0.43$\,pp, and $\sigma(\text{DR\%}) \leq 0.08$\,pp.
29	The constrained decoding DR\% reduction ($-$2.06\,pp for Qwen, $-$1.01\,pp for Llama) exceeds this variance by ${>}12\times$ (signal-to-noise ratio ${>}$\,12), confirming that observed improvements reflect genuine method effects rather than run-to-run noise.
30	We therefore report single-run results without error bars; bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 iterations) are provided for F1 metrics.
31	
32	\begin{table}[h]
33	\centering
34	\caption{Cross-seed variance for LoRA fine-tuned models on SciERC (551 documents, greedy decoding, seeds 42/123/456). Values: mean $\pm$ population $\sigma$ across three seeds. Seeds 123 and 456 produce identical outputs on the same GPU; all observed $\sigma$ reflects cross-server floating-point divergence.}
35	\label{tab:seed-variance}
36	\small
37	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
38	\begin{tabular}{@{}lllccc@{}}
39	\toprule
40	Model & Dataset & Mode & E-F1 & R-F1 & DR\% \\
```

**并行（维度1）**：你 30 分钟没有主动动作了，最近都是在被动回复运维的飞书消息。Agent、Reviewer、Worker 全部空闲。匿名仓库等 token 没问题，但 final freeze 之后还有事可以推：ARR 提交流程准备（supplementary 打包、checklist 填写、PDF 最终检查）、提交前 camera-ready 格式确认，这些不依赖 token，现在就能让 Agent 做起来。

**论文质量（维度4）**：DDL 还有约 44 小时，论文 frozen 了，你评估当前 solid Findings-level 3.0-3.5。这个分数你满意吗？Reviewer 说有 2 个被低估数据点你已经用了一个（FP rate in intro），另一个 "1/514 FN blocked" 落地了吗？还有没有零风险的文字层面改进可以再争 0.5 分？

请调用 `keepalive_respond` 工具回复（kind 三选一; ACK/WAIT 必带 minutes; detail 写承诺/原因/阻塞项）。

394	The grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\,pp (4/4 settings).
395	The semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 41--69\% on SciERC (Qwen $-$69\%, Llama $-$41\%) and 2--75\% on DocRED (Qwen $-$75\%, Llama $-$2\%); the near-zero Llama DocRED reduction reflects a near-floor baseline (DR${=}$0.49\%, 80 violations in 16{,}074 triples), where the semantic layer has minimal room to improve.
396	Adding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\,$\leq$\,0.21\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.
397	F1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across S1 vs.\ unconstrained is $-$0.30\,pp (Qwen DocRED).

Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.\nEntity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors (28\\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nOur empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.\nWe do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nOur approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex}; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nEvaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\nWhile composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_70b_ablation}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\\input{appendix_wikievents_pilot}\n\\input{appendix_zs_crossmodel}\n\\input{appendix_quantization}\n\\input{appendix_gollie_comparison}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **OntoCoDec**: Two-layer constrained decoding framework (grammar via XGrammar + semantic via TypeConstraintProcessor) for IE type violation enforcement
   - **Four violation types**: DR (domain-range), RV (raw vocabulary), ET (entity type), CF (cross-field)
   - **DR→CF trade-off**: Greedy S1 reduces DR to ≤0.21% but introduces CF (≤1.4% fine-tuned). Total violation reduction = 26-52% (greedy S1 FT). Beam k=4 eliminates all
   - **Severity-framing**: DR is irrecoverable, CF is correctable via TCR — used in Abstract instead of total violation percentages
   - **Non-additive layer interaction**: Grammar alone 3% DR reduction, semantic alone 24%, composition yields 94% at 8B
   - **Scale-dependent behavior**: Semantic-only achieves 84% DR reduction at 70B vs 24% at 8B
   - **FP rate of removed triples**: 97.6-100% are false positives — key "lossless enforcement" argument added to intro
   - **EMNLP 2026 format**: 8-page main text limit, ≤200 word abstract, Limitations/Ethics don't count toward pages, appendix unlimited

Type-Constrained Information Extraction\\\\via Compiled Schema Constraints}\n\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\input{abstract}\n\n% === §1 Introduction ===\n\\input{section1_introduction_draft}\n\n% === §2 Background ===\n\\input{section2_background}\n\n% === §3 Method ===\n\\input{section3_method_draft}\n\n% === §4 Constraint Composition ===\n\\section{Constraint Composition}\n\\label{sec:composition}\n\\input{section4_composition_draft}\n\n% === §5 Experiments ===\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\input{section3_violation_landscape}\n\n\\input{section5_scierc_draft}\n\\input{section5_wikievents_draft}\n\\input{section5_runtime}\n\n% === §6 Related Work ===\n\\input{section6_related_work}\n\n% === §7 Discussion and Conclusion ===\n\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\input{section7_conclusion_compressed}\n\n% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint scope.}\nCF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.\nEntity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors (28\\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nOur empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.\nWe do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nOur approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex}; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nEvaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\nWhile composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix     "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at F1 40--46\\%, converting irrecoverable type errors to correctable cross-field inconsistencies (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$ latency.\nIn zero-shot settings, DR reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; CF increases are concentrated in hallucinated entities (58.1\\%) and decrease with scale and fine-tuning.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },

        "content": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 74\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 reduces DR to near-zero: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at R-F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); 97.6--100\\% of removed triples are false positives (\\S\\ref{sec:downstream-impact}). Beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B on both RE datasets (1.39$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) in zero-shot settings; Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B additionally under LoRA fine-tuning, demonstrating DR reduction to $\\leq$0.21\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.3\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework grounded in the observation that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars---grammar-only approaches face state spaces of $6^{17}$--$6^{19}$ for typical IE schemas (Proposition~1; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Preliminary single-family scale exploration on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). At 8B, beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ greedy S1 latency); $k{=}4$ achieves DR${=}$0\\% with near-zero CF at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 31,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 31
      }
